My wife would notice before I would—she’s always been the one to say, “That jacket’s too loose,” or “Those cuffs need doing.”\n\nFunny thing, I’ve still got suits upstairs from when I was working, and some of them never fit properly even then. Tailors are a strange breed, aren’t they? But since the sleep got bad, no, I haven’t had a clear sense that my clothes are fitting differently.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, to the point where others have noticed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not that anyone’s said to me. My wife hasn’t remarked on me looking thinner or anything like that.\n\nMind you, people don’t always say these things, do they? Years ago my aunt lost weight and everyone danced around it for weeks before anyone mentioned it… but no, in my case, I can’t say there’s been a significant loss that others have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I haven’t… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m properly up—I think it would almost be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up at all.\n\nThat scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left on my own. I felt rather ashamed saying it, actually. My father’s generation never talked about these things—just “pull yourself together” and all that nonsense—but yes, it frightened me. I didn’t want to be alone with that feeling.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to act on those thoughts?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"There are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply did not wake up, and I frightened myself enough to tell my wife I should not be left alone.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not in that sense. I haven’t sat there thinking, “I’ll do this or that.” It’s more… there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up.

And that frightened me, really. Enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I ought to be left alone. She was in the kitchen at the time, I remember—funny the little details that stick, isn’t it? But no, I haven’t worked out how I’d end my life. It’s the feeling that scared me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just… come loose somehow. I used to be a very regular sleeper, or at least I thought I was—my wife used to joke I could fall asleep during the evening news, though I always said the news was enough to put anyone out.\n\nBut lately I’ll sleep two, maybe three hours, and then I’m up, wide awake, making plans, writing things down, feeling like I’m ready to get going. And now it’s changed again—I’m lying there before dawn with my stomach all tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything particularly stressful happening in your life around the time your sleep started to change?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after I lost money in an investment—more than I should have risked, honestly. After that, for about five months I wasn’t really living like my usual retired self. Everything became about trying to fix it, undo the damage somehow.\n\nI kept thinking if I just made the right call, talked to the right person, moved things around… you know, like when I was younger and handled problems by just working harder. Though retirement is funny that way—you think you’ve left all that behind, and then one bad decision pulls you right back into that old mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, exactly. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through—my old boss used to say I could worry a spreadsheet into behaving, which I never knew if that was a compliment or not.\n\nBut “worrying more”… I don’t know. Some mornings I do catch myself turning things over, but then I’ll get sidetracked thinking about whether I wrote something down, or I’ll remember some errand from two weeks ago. So it’s hard to separate worry from just… my mind not staying put.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or activities you've been avoiding lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Not avoiding in the sense of being afraid of them, exactly. It’s more like I look at the things I used to enjoy and just can’t make myself start.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, you know, wandering from room to room, especially the photography exhibits. I had a camera too—still do, a decent one—and I’d cook afterward sometimes, make a whole day of it. And bridge, I used to enjoy bridge, though one of the men at the table always overbid and then acted surprised when we went down.\n\nBut lately I look at the camera, or think about going to the museum, or even sitting down at the bridge table, and I just feel… nothing. Or not nothing—more like the effort of pretending I’m interested feels too much. So I don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Empty is a funny word… I mean, I look around the house sometimes and it’s quiet in a way that feels heavier than it used to, but then I’ll get thinking about how the living room needs repainting, or whether I ever fixed that loose handle on the kitchen drawer.\n\nSo… most of the time? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it. I just know things haven’t felt quite right.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that used to give you pleasure, but no longer do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there are.\n\nThe museum, for one. I used to spend whole afternoons there, especially in the photography exhibits. I’d take my camera sometimes too, or come home and cook something properly—not just heat something up. And bridge, even with all the nonsense about bidding conventions, I did enjoy it.\n\nLately, though, I look at the camera or think about sitting at the bridge table, and there’s just… nothing there. Except the thought of how much effort it would take to act like I’m enjoying myself. So I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. I keep going over that investment decision in my head. It’s ridiculous, really, because at the time I thought I was being careful—I’ve always been the responsible one, the one who checked the figures, kept the files in order, made sure the bills were paid.\n\nAnd now I sit there thinking I’ve ruined my wife’s security. Made a fool of the whole life I spent trying to be sensible. I’ll be brushing my teeth or looking for the newspaper and suddenly I’m back at that decision again, like if I replay it enough I’ll find the moment where I should’ve known better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer able to experience pleasure from activities that you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I used to spend whole afternoons at the museum—there was this one photography exhibit, I remember the way they lit the prints, not too harsh, which makes all the difference, really—and I could lose track of time.\n\nAnd my own camera, cooking something decent, even bridge… I used to get something out of all that. Lately I just look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the bridge table, and I feel nothing. Just the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, to be honest. I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale.\n\nMy trousers… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. But then, I wear the same old belt I’ve had for years, and those things stretch, don’t they? My wife used to say I never noticed if my shirts were fraying until they were practically falling apart.\n\nSo… significant weight loss? I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or wanting to harm yourself lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… not exactly—well, I suppose it is that. There are mornings when I wake up, or before I’m fully awake really, and I think it would be a relief if I simply hadn’t. Like not having to face the day, the accounts, all the small things piled up.\n\nI didn’t want to frighten my wife, she’s had enough to deal with, and she always keeps the breakfast things in the same cupboard even though I’ve told her the other one would be easier—but I did tell her. I scared myself enough that I said I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that neatly. “Cope” is one of those words people use and it sounds sensible, but it can mean anything from not shouting at the bank clerk to remembering where you put the kettle.\n\nMost of the time? I suppose I don’t really know. Some days I think I’m managing, and then something very small—like a drawer sticking, or the post arriving late—feels much larger than it ought to. But then I’ve always been particular about things being in the right place. My father was like that too, had a whole system for his tools in the garage.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear answer. It feels muddled, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes… often enough that it’s started to frighten me. It’s mostly in the mornings, that thought that it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up.\n\nI don’t sit there planning anything exactly, not like… well, I don’t know. But it was strong enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. And I don’t like worrying her—she worries about the boiler, the bins, all sorts—but I had to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. My wife notices it more than I do, probably. She says I’m either pacing through the house, shuffling bits of paper about like I’m late for some board meeting—which is ridiculous really, I haven’t had a board meeting in years—or I’m just… stuck.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table, very still, and she’ll ask me something simple, like whether I want tea, and it takes me ages to answer. Not because it’s a difficult question. I just seem to be somewhere else, or moving through treacle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. My wife says I can sit at the kitchen table so still it’s almost like I’ve been switched off, and if she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer.\n\nBut then other times I’m up and down the house, pacing about with papers in my hands as if I’m late for some board meeting. Funny, because I’m not working now, haven’t been for ages… though I still keep old folders in the study, which I really should sort out. But yes, slower sometimes. Noticeably.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since your sleep problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, to be honest. I haven’t been keeping track of the scales or anything like that. My wife might have a better sense of it—she notices if my trousers are hanging differently, that sort of thing.\n\nI mean, I’ve never been one for weighing myself. Even at university I remember there was this fellow who weighed himself every morning like it was a religious duty, which always seemed odd to me… but no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a big drop in weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently since your sleep problems started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no. My shirts and trousers seem… well, much the same, I think. My wife would notice before I would—she’s always been the one to say, “That jacket’s too loose,” or “Those cuffs need doing.”\n\nFunny thing, I’ve still got suits upstairs from when I was working, and some of them never fit properly even then. Tailors are a strange breed, aren’t they? But since the sleep got bad, no, I haven’t had a clear sense that my clothes are fitting differently.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, to the point where others have noticed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not that anyone’s said to me. My wife hasn’t remarked on me looking thinner or anything like that.\n\nMind you, people don’t always say these things, do they? But no, I haven’t worked out how I’d end my life. It’s the feeling that scared me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just… come loose somehow. I used to be a very regular sleeper, or at least I thought I was—my wife used to joke I could fall asleep during the evening news, though I always said the news was enough to put anyone out.\n\nBut lately I’ll sleep two, maybe three hours, and then I’m up, wide awake, making plans, writing things down, feeling like I’m ready to get going. And now it’s changed again—I’m lying there before dawn with my stomach all tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. It’s wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything particularly stressful happening in your life around the time your sleep started to change?\n\nPatient: Yes. It started after I lost money in an investment—more than I should have risked, honestly. After that, for about five months I wasn’t really living like my usual retired self. Everything became about trying to fix it, undo the damage somehow.\n\nI kept thinking if I just made the right call, talked to the right person, moved things around… you know, like when I was younger and handled problems by just working harder. Though retirement is funny that way—you think you’ve left all that behind, and then one bad decision pulls you right back into that old mode.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things more than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, exactly. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through—my old boss used to say I could worry a spreadsheet into behaving, which I never knew if that was a compliment or not.\n\nBut “worrying more”… I don’t know. Some mornings I do catch myself turning things over, but then I’ll get sidetracked thinking about whether I wrote something down, or I’ll remember some errand from two weeks ago. So it’s hard to separate worry from just… my mind not staying put.\n\nDoctor: Are there things or activities you've been avoiding lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. Not avoiding in the sense of being afraid of them, exactly. It’s more like I look at the things I used to enjoy and just can’t make myself start.\n\nI used to spend whole afternoons at the museum, you know, wandering from room to room, especially the photography exhibits. I had a camera too—still do, a decent one—and I’d cook afterward sometimes, make a whole day of it. And bridge, I used to enjoy bridge, though one of the men at the table always overbid and then acted surprised when we went down.\n\nBut lately I look at the camera, or think about going to the museum, or even sitting down at the bridge table, and I just feel… nothing. Or not nothing—more like the effort of pretending I’m interested feels too much. So I don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. Empty is a funny word… I mean, I look around the house sometimes and it’s quiet in a way that feels heavier than it used to, but then I’ll get thinking about how the living room needs repainting, or whether I ever fixed that loose handle on the kitchen drawer.\n\nSo… most of the time? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to exaggerate it. I just know things haven’t felt quite right.\n\nDoctor: Are there things that used to give you pleasure, but no longer do?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, there are.\n\nThe museum, for one. I used to spend whole afternoons there, especially in the photography exhibits. I’d take my camera sometimes too, or come home and cook something properly—not just heat something up. And bridge, even with all the nonsense about bidding conventions, I did enjoy it.\n\nLately, though, I look at the camera or think about sitting at the bridge table, and there’s just… nothing there. Except the thought of how much effort it would take to act like I’m enjoying myself. So I don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. I keep going over that investment decision in my head. It’s ridiculous, really, because at the time I thought I was being careful—I’ve always been the responsible one, the one who checked the figures, kept the files in order, made sure the bills were paid.\n\nAnd now I sit there thinking I’ve ruined my wife’s security. Made a fool of the whole life I spent trying to be sensible. I’ll be brushing my teeth or looking for the newspaper and suddenly I’m back at that decision again, like if I replay it enough I’ll find the moment where I should’ve known better.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're no longer able to experience pleasure from activities that you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I used to spend whole afternoons at the museum—there was this one photography exhibit, I remember the way they lit the prints, not too harsh, which makes all the difference, really—and I could lose track of time.\n\nAnd my own camera, cooking something decent, even bridge… I used to get something out of all that. Lately I just look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the bridge table, and I feel nothing. Just the effort it would take to pretend I’m enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, to be honest. I haven’t really been keeping track of the scale.\n\nMy trousers… I don’t think they’re hanging off me or anything like that. But then, I wear the same old belt I’ve had for years, and those things stretch, don’t they? My wife used to say I never noticed if my shirts were fraying until they were practically falling apart.\n\nSo… significant weight loss? I don’t know. I haven’t noticed anything definite.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or wanting to harm yourself lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… not exactly—well, I suppose it is that. There are mornings when I wake up, or before I’m fully awake really, and I think it would be a relief if I simply hadn’t. Like not having to face the day, the accounts, all the small things piled up.\n\nI didn’t want to frighten my wife, she’s had enough to deal with, and she always keeps the breakfast things in the same cupboard even though I’ve told her the other one would be easier—but I did tell her. I scared myself enough that I said I shouldn’t be left alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're struggling to cope with your emotions most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that neatly. “Cope” is one of those words people use and it sounds sensible, but it can mean anything from not shouting at the bank clerk to remembering where you put the kettle.\n\nMost of the time? I suppose I don’t really know. Some days I think I’m managing, and then something very small—like a drawer sticking, or the post arriving late—feels much larger than it ought to. But then I’ve always been particular about things being in the right place. My father was like that too, had a whole system for his tools in the garage.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a clear answer. It feels muddled, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts about death frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes… often enough that it’s started to frighten me. It’s mostly in the mornings, that thought that it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up.\n\nI don’t sit there planning anything exactly, not like… well, I don’t know. But it was strong enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. And I don’t like worrying her—she worries about the boiler, the bins, all sorts—but I had to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. My wife notices it more than I do, probably. She says I’m either pacing through the house, shuffling bits of paper about like I’m late for some board meeting—which is ridiculous really, I haven’t had a board meeting in years—or I’m just… stuck.\n\nI’ll sit at the kitchen table, very still, and she’ll ask me something simple, like whether I want tea, and it takes me ages to answer. Not because it’s a difficult question. I just seem to be somewhere else, or moving through treacle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. My wife says I can sit at the kitchen table so still it’s almost like I’ve been switched off, and if she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer.\n\nBut then other times I’m up and down the house, pacing about with papers in my hands as if I’m late for some board meeting. Funny, because I’m not working now, haven’t been for ages… though I still keep old folders in the study, which I really should sort out. But yes, slower sometimes. I haven’t been keeping track of the scales or anything like that. My wife might have a better sense of it—she notices if my trousers are hanging differently, that sort of thing.\n\nI mean, I’ve never been one for weighing myself. Even at university I remember there was this fellow who weighed himself every morning like it was a religious duty, which always seemed odd to me… but no, I can’t say I’ve noticed a big drop in weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently since your sleep problems started?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I’ve noticed that, no. My shirts and trousers seem… well, much the same, I think. My wife would notice before I would—she’s always been the one to say, “That jacket’s too loose,” or “Those cuffs need doing.”\n\nFunny thing, I’ve still got suits upstairs from when I was working, and some of them never fit properly even then. Tailors are a strange breed, aren’t they? But since the sleep got bad, no, I haven’t had a clear sense that my clothes are fitting differently.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently, to the point where others have noticed?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so—not that anyone’s said to me. My wife hasn’t remarked on me looking thinner or anything like that.\n\nMind you, people don’t always say these things, do they? Years ago my aunt lost weight and everyone danced around it for weeks before anyone mentioned it… but no, in my case, I can’t say there’s been a significant loss that others have noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about not waking up?\n\nPatient: I haven’t… I don’t think I’ve sat down and made a plan, if that’s what you mean. It’s more that some mornings I wake up—or before I’m properly up—I think it would almost be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up at all.\n\nThat scared me enough that I told my wife I shouldn’t be left on my own. I felt rather ashamed saying it, actually. My father’s generation never talked about these things—just “pull yourself together” and all that nonsense—but yes, it frightened me. I didn’t want to be alone with that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would end your life if you decided to act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, not in that sense. I haven’t sat there thinking, “I’ll do this or that.” It’s more… there are mornings when I think it would be a relief if I simply didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd that frightened me, really. Enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I ought to be left alone. She was in the kitchen at the time, I remember—funny the little details that stick, isn’t it? But no, I haven’t worked out how I’d end my life. It’s the feeling that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about methods or ways you would use to harm yourself?\n\nPatient: No… not methods, really. I haven’t been thinking, “I’d use this” or “I’d do it that way.” It’s more that some mornings I wake up and think… it would be a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up.\n\nAnd that shook me. I mean, I remember telling my wife I didn’t think I should be left on my own, which is not an easy thing to say. Funny, you spend years being the one who’s meant to cope, and then suddenly you’re saying something like that over breakfast. But no, I haven’t worked out ways to harm myself. It’s the not wanting to wake up feeling that frightened me."
